# Unfilter digest 02 — baby and toddler sleep

2026-11-16. Build: `mamivio.com/research/sleep-baby/`.

This share contains **1,124 quote records**: 431 in `voice/reddit-ur09.jsonl`, 349 in `voice/reddit-ur16.jsonl`, and 344 in `voice/reddit-ur17.jsonl`. Every record was read sequentially, including the ends of all three files, alongside the README, current findings and each ledger’s **What these posts add** section. The ledgers are `sources/reddit-reading-ur09.md`, `sources/reddit-reading-ur16.md` and `sources/reddit-reading-ur17.md`.

These are selected quotes recovered from historical exclusions. **Counts describe quotes, never people or prevalence.** An ID is not a distinct parent, a vote for a method or an independently verified medical outcome. Several IDs continue the same post or follow-up. Retired ID gaps are not missing records. No exact quote text is duplicated across the 1,124 source records; excerpts can nevertheless overlap or concern the same account.

The current findings already restore medical uncertainty, dangerous dozing, distress, coercion and changing attitudes toward shared sleep. This digest does not announce those broad themes as new. Its main additions are the separate pumping clock, fathers’ own crises, birth-trauma and accessibility constraints, physical violence during a handoff, reported infant loss, buying awake overnight care, medication-start coordination, and cases where successful bedtime or infant sleep does not resolve the actual problem.

The comparisons below quote the **current findings’ wording** explicitly. Those short excerpts are not new voice records. Every block labelled with a share ID reproduces that record’s complete `quote` field, including spelling, punctuation, line breaks, Markdown escapes and the speaker’s denials or qualifications. Medical, developmental, safety and causal claims remain the speaker’s claims. Nothing is withheld or softened because of its subject.

Each record has one primary pattern for coverage. Strong or distinctive passages are reproduced in full; less distinctive corroboration is listed after the relevant pattern. Some full passages recur in counterexamples or where a second pattern needs the complete sequence; those are repeated evidence, not additional observations. The Money and Requests indexes point back to complete quotations in the patterns.

## Patterns and changes to the current findings

| Pattern | Current section | Relationship |
|---|---|---|
| 1. Sleep distress includes self-harm, suicidal thoughts, verbal aggression and reported physical acts toward a child. | §§6.4, 9.1 and 10 | Extends |
| 2. The parent’s recovery has its own course, and better infant sleep or a brief break does not establish recovery. | §§9.1, 10 and 17.7 | Confirms and extends |
| 3. Fathers describe their own crisis, symptoms, care decisions and impaired functioning as well as concern for their partners. | §§5.1, 9.1 and especially 9.2 | Contradicts the narrow framing; extends the existing first-person accounts |
| 4. A night shift may be absent, unusable or frightening even when another adult is physically present. | §§5.1–5.2, 9.6, 10 and 17.9 | Extends |
| 5. Pumping, painful feeding and breast health can prevent adult sleep even when someone else can cover the baby. | §§3, 5.1, 9.1 and 10; add an explicit feeding-clock subsection | Extends |
| 6. A parent’s medication course can create an immediate need to redesign sleeping, feeding and partner shifts. | §§3, 9.1 and 10; new coordination subsection | Extends |
| 7. Pain, birth trauma, injuries and recovery can rule out the very soothing or sleeping arrangement that works for the baby. | §§4.5, 6.2, 9.1 and 10 | Extends |
| 8. Adult attention, sensory and motor difficulties change whether ordinary sleep advice is usable. | §§6.2, 9.1–9.2 and 10 | Extends |
| 9. Parents describe accidental sleep, falls and monitoring practices alongside knowing the stated sleep rules. | §§4.3, 4.5, 6.4 and 9.1 | Extends |
| 10. One parent reports an infant’s death in a cosleeping accident and grief about another pregnancy. | §6.4 and a new bereavement subsection connected to §9.3 | Extends |
| 11. Shared sleep can be loved, used temporarily, ineffective, painful or unwanted even when the baby sleeps well. | §§5.5, 6.3 and 9.5 | Confirms and extends |
| 12. Night weaning, ending comfort feeding and ending breastfeeding are different decisions, constrained by growth, pain and upcoming events. | §§3, 6.1–6.2 and 9.5 | Extends |
| 13. Feeding mechanics, refusal and nursing behavior make removing an association more complicated than changing the final bedtime step. | §§3, 4.5 and 6.1–6.2 | Extends |
| 14. Upright holding, feeding difficulty and reflux workups can create the sleep association parents are later told to remove. | §§3, 4.5, 9.1 and 17.2 | Extends |
| 15. Allergies and itching can prevent independent sleep and change what parents consider an acceptable crying method. | §§3, 4.5, 6.2 and 17.9 | Extends |
| 16. Pain relief, sleep remedies and teething explanations produce mixed observations rather than one reliable explanation of waking. | §§3, 4.6 and 17.2–17.9 | Confirms and extends |
| 17. Normal results, low-range iron, referral decisions and new symptoms leave the medical explanation unresolved in different ways. | §§3, 6.2, 8 and 17.2 | Extends with contrary evidence |
| 18. Illness changes the night’s care workload, but recovery and sleep do not reliably move together. | §§2, 3, 5.4 and 17.2 | Confirms and extends |
| 19. Seeking appropriate help can fail at referral, routing, timing or trust before any sleep plan is tried. | §§3, 8, 9.6 and 10 | Extends |
| 20. Paying for sleep help can mean buying an awake caregiver, whose failure can destroy trust despite a partial refund. | §§4.1, 8, 10 and 17.3; add a night-care service subsection | Extends |
| 21. Independent bedtime, independent naps, night resettling, feeding and adult recovery are separate outcomes. | §§2, 4.4–4.5, 6.1 and 17.2 | Extends |
| 22. Parents stop or modify attempts because of the child’s physical distress, injury risk, their own limit or a rule they cannot apply. | §§4.5, 6.1 and 17.6–17.9 | Confirms and extends |
| 23. Schedules and methods can stop fitting after illness, travel, development or a nap change, and the old method may no longer work. | §§2, 4.4, 6.1 and 17.2 | Confirms and extends |
| 24. Parents want workable next steps while uncertain whether the issue is timing, separation, regulation or the method itself. | §§4.4, 6.1–6.2 and 17.6 | Confirms and extends |
| 25. Parents cannot apply age guidance without deciding how prematurity, this child’s cues and actual sleep change the comparison. | §§2 and 6.2, with contrary evidence in §17.6 | Extends |
| 26. Sleepwear, sleep spaces and monitors are repeated experiments whose fit, lifespan and usability affect value. | §§4.6, 5.4 and 8 | Confirms and extends |
| 27. Daycare and relatives can constrain naps and care, provide relief or be difficult to trust. | §§5.3–5.4, 6.2 and 9.6 | Confirms and extends |
| 28. Space, ventilation, heat, siblings and travel determine which otherwise plausible sleep arrangements can be attempted. | §§4.5, 8 and 9.6 | Confirms and extends |
| 29. Sleep hardship changes plans for another child and the preparation parents seek, as well as whether an old method fits the next baby. | §§9.1 and 9.3, with a preparation subsection in §6.1 | Extends |
| 30. Parents question advice systems and harm claims, including chatbots, rather than simply needing more instructions. | §§3, 4.2–4.3, 7 and 17.5–17.6 | Confirms and extends |

### 1. Sleep distress includes self-harm, suicidal thoughts, verbal aggression and reported physical acts toward a child.

**Current findings:** §§6.4, 9.1 and 10. **Relationship:** Extends.

**Current conclusion:** “The earlier account of guilt and lost identity omitted reported harshness and harm.”

**What should change or stay:** Retain the current recognition of rage and harm, and make the new actions explicit: hitting oneself, renewed self-harm, wishing not to wake, and suicidal thoughts; yelling at a baby; yanking a playpen so a child hits a hard toy; tossing a baby onto a bed; and the reported blanket and pillow incidents. Keep the speaker’s explanations, denials, durations and guilt with the act. Unwanted thoughts explicitly denied as actions are different evidence from reported actions. The practical coping request is how to regulate while holding a baby at 3am, rather than a generic instruction to take a break. These reports do not establish that training treats the parent’s condition.

**Further supporting IDs:** [sbu09-0018], [sbu09-0147], [sbu09-0155], [sbu09-0203], [sbu09-0383], [sbu16-0001], [sbu16-0069], [sbu16-0259], [sbu16-0307], [sbu17-0095], [sbu17-0127], [sbu17-0130], [sbu17-0164].

### 2. The parent’s recovery has its own course, and better infant sleep or a brief break does not establish recovery.

**Current findings:** §§9.1, 10 and 17.7. **Relationship:** Confirms and extends.

**Current conclusion:** “It does not support promising that the parent's own sleep will come back when the baby's does;”

**What should change or stay:** Preserve this qualification prominently. Add nonrestorative sleep despite early bedtime, normal blood tests despite exhaustion, increased anxiety after sleep improves, and worsening mental health after the baby starts sleeping through. A few nanny-assisted mornings or dates can feel good without resolving burnout. Conversely, retain accounts of restored exercise, social life and functioning. One author describes good infant sleep and says marital distress is unrelated to the baby; another reports only one or two night feeds while hoping not to wake. Wake counts alone cannot stand in for the parent’s state.

### 3. Fathers describe their own crisis, symptoms, care decisions and impaired functioning as well as concern for their partners.

**Current findings:** §§5.1, 9.1 and especially 9.2. **Relationship:** Contradicts the narrow framing; extends the existing first-person accounts.

**Current conclusion:** “They frame the pain through the wife:”

**What should change or stay:** Replace that generalization with a wider father portrait. A father reports self-harm, renewed suicidal ideation and PPD during pumping/feeding shifts; others describe contempt, inability to bond, slurring, getting lost while driving, work impairment and willingness to pay for a dad therapist. A father also reports yelling at his wife in front of the child and feeling guilty. These accounts coexist with, rather than erase, fathers describing their partner’s pain. A plan a father can execute is only one of the needs represented.

**[sbu09-0253]**

<!-- voice:sbu09-0253 -->
> We had our baby girl 2 weeks ago today, and have been home with her for a week. So far mum and I have been working in shifts, and feeding her and pumping every 3h. Each feed takes about an hour, so neither of us had gotten more than 2-3h of straight sleep in a fortnight. When we’re on shift, the baby won’t go down on her cot, so we need to hold her in our arms, so we can’t sleep,
> 
> I’m not coping well, I had mental health issues and now I’m getting PPD hitting me like a ton of bricks. I’ve self harmed and started having suicidal ideation again. On top of everything, we’re both unemployed (I work as a contractor), and need to look for work starting tomorrow. 
> 
> Does anyone have any advice on how we can change things to be sustainable? I’m really desperate here.

### 4. A night shift may be absent, unusable or frightening even when another adult is physically present.

**Current findings:** §§5.1–5.2, 9.6, 10 and 17.9. **Relationship:** Extends.

**Current conclusion:** “These are different from two willing caregivers needing the same script.”

**What should change or stay:** Keep this distinction and add the concrete barriers: thrown pacifiers, slammed doors, fear of leaving a baby with the partner, choking the mother during a handoff while holding the baby, nighttime verbal abuse, and an unused second care setup bought to enable promised mornings. Promised coverage may repeatedly wake the mother for updates and yield less sleep. A partner’s job, illness, insomnia or medication restrictions can also constrain coverage. The accounts of cooperative and effective caregivers remain counter-evidence; do not collapse every difficult handoff into unwillingness or violence.

My mom can only come help every 2 weeks or so due to her work schedule and I don’t have friends nearby so I essentially resort to catnaps while holding my kid (don’t come for me I’m very well aware of the dangers).

### 5. Pumping, painful feeding and breast health can prevent adult sleep even when someone else can cover the baby.

**Current findings:** §§3, 5.1, 9.1 and 10; add an explicit feeding-clock subsection. **Relationship:** Extends.

**Current conclusion:** “The plan request remains, but age, schedule and stated values do not describe the full fit problem.”

**What should change or stay:** Name the separate maternal clock: three-hour pumping instructions, oversupply, drying supply gradually because of feared infection, engorgement or mastitis concerns, and independent baby sleep that falls outside the pumping window. One parent pumps milk the baby cannot tolerate; another cannot nurse or pump effectively enough to leave for an evening. The 3am self-injury account includes scratching or biting nails until bleeding to distract from latch pain. External pressure to continue and conflicting professional advice are part of the record. Longer infant stretches or hired hands alone do not remove these constraints.

**[sbu09-0095]**

<!-- voice:sbu09-0095 -->
> It’s not even just that my baby won’t let me put her down. She also wants fed about every 2-2.5 hours, so even though she’s exceeded her birth weight and we technically can let her sleep longer, she won’t, not that it’s even relevant since that sleep is in my arms. I also have to pump my massive oversupply of breast milk at minimum every 4 hours, which I can’t even feed my baby because she doesn’t tolerate it, so it’s just another 24 hour demanding schedule that I have to follow just to fill up my deep freezer and maybe donate later or something. 
> 
> I’m just so frustrated. Between the feeding schedule and pumping schedule that both have to be set very rigidity, and baby being so difficult to set down, I just feel like sleep is impossible. On the off chance that I do get her to sleep independently, that window will not be longer than an hour or so and if it doesn’t fall between pumping then it’s useless to try and sleep.
<!-- /voice -->

### 6. A parent’s medication course can create an immediate need to redesign sleeping, feeding and partner shifts.

**Current findings:** §§3, 9.1 and 10; new coordination subsection. **Relationship:** Extends.

**Current conclusion:** “The restored accounts include pain, recovery, surgery, a seizure diagnosis, tremors and other reported physical effects.”

**What should change or stay:** Add the planned Zurzuvae course as a distinct logistics problem: anticipated heavy sleep, chest sleeping that has been the only workable arrangement, three-hourly pumping or possible feeds, stored milk, a floor mattress, husband’s work and the feared midnight-to-6am shift. Preserve the mother’s proposed schedule as a proposal and her expectation about the medication as her account. No successful outcome or verified medication instruction is present in these three excerpts.

### 7. Pain, birth trauma, injuries and recovery can rule out the very soothing or sleeping arrangement that works for the baby.

**Current findings:** §§4.5, 6.2, 9.1 and 10. **Relationship:** Extends.

**Current conclusion:** “a caregiver may be physically unable to sustain rocking, holding or a walking-based solution.”

**What should change or stay:** Add specific limits: upper-back sprain forces an abrupt end to babywearing; a broken arm prevents lifting or restraining; shoulder pain makes bedsharing intolerable; back injury and partner surgical recovery remove both usual holders; incision/back pain prevents adult sleep between feeds. Birth trauma is not merely a general view about crying: one parent says crying returns her to the delivery room and refuses training for that reason. Bedsharing, a recliner and a floor bed can each help one part of care while worsening adult pain. The words about taking or fearing medication remain the parents’ accounts.

### 8. Adult attention, sensory and motor difficulties change whether ordinary sleep advice is usable.

**Current findings:** §§6.2, 9.1–9.2 and 10. **Relationship:** Extends.

**Current conclusion:** “Health, feeding, physical capacity, mental state, available help, living space and the cost of professional assessment all appear in the restored words”

**What should change or stay:** Make fit concrete rather than treating these as background labels: the parent who returns a carrier and cannot imitate wrap instructions; the father who says medication is necessary for functioning but worsens sleep; the father describing ADHD becoming impairing under chronic sleep loss; and the partner whose hallucination trigger makes white noise problematic. Another family finds penetrating noise panic-inducing. The author discussing a calm newborn during an unresolved autism assessment explicitly rejects a universal calm-baby/autism link; preserve that counterexample and do not infer a diagnostic sleep profile.

<!-- voice:sbu16-0014 -->

My husband showed me how it works but as I said I’m just not confident holding her with one hand and switching hands all while focusing on getting the carrier secure. However, if I’m missing a lot of the blocks, even meds won’t help my head get above water. So I need to really focus on taking care of myself, as a part of being able to take care of my family.

### 9. Parents describe accidental sleep, falls and monitoring practices alongside knowing the stated sleep rules.

**Current findings:** §§4.3, 4.5, 6.4 and 9.1. **Relationship:** Extends.

**Current conclusion:** “These are concrete triggers for asking for change: falling asleep while holding the baby, finding the baby face down in a pillow, a near road accident and a demanding clinical shift.”

**What should change or stay:** Add the implementation gap explicitly: both adults fall asleep during shifts; bedsharing is feared because of deep sleep; a parent sleeps through an hour of crying, then avoids nighttime sleep; exhausted parents report actual falls. A monitor or wearable can reassure without making a transfer or waking workable, and a toddler monitor may be too quiet for the exhausted caregiver. Include active parental positioning, chest and chair sleep and the reasons speakers give, without adopting their safety claims as findings. Some fear concerns their own anxiety, others follow an event that happened; keep those apart.

### 10. One parent reports an infant’s death in a cosleeping accident and grief about another pregnancy.

**Current findings:** §6.4 and a new bereavement subsection connected to §9.3. **Relationship:** Extends.

**Current conclusion:** “The full words connect breathing checks, fear of infant death, guilt about both sleep arrangements, fear of lasting harm and dread that the next regression will repeat an earlier collapse.”

**What should change or stay:** Do not file this only as anticipatory death anxiety. The parent says the death happened, gives the baby’s age, and describes guilt, longing to mother again and fear about future babies. Preserve the account and its attribution; the excerpt does not supply an independently verified cause or circumstances beyond the parent’s description.

**[sbu16-0359]**

<!-- voice:sbu16-0359 -->
> i lost my son 16 days ago, he was only 14 days old, (due to co sleeping accident i talk about it in another post)
<!-- /voice -->

**[sbu16-0360]**

<!-- voice:sbu16-0360 -->
> my brain feels so guilty for even thinking when can i try again, and then i say how could i EVER have another baby when i want him. then i say why the fuck am i even deserving of that!!!! but i know i crave being a mom more than anything... that's what i was supposed to be doing right now is loving my beautiful son. the question is when's too early? i feel so fucking guilty for having these thoughts, i don't want to betray him, bc NOTHING can ever replace him, every second my feelings are something different. i can't believe this our reality, i can't believe my sweet son doesn't get to be enduring the love my partner and i had for our little family, i feel so sick that he can't experience life again, he should be here...  
> then i think whenever of the fear i'm going to feel the rest of my life when i have other babies and how ill always worry something will happen no matter what i do... idk if any of this makes sense. it's been a hard day. he would of been one month old today.... it will never feel real.
<!-- /voice -->

**Further supporting IDs:** none; all assigned evidence for this pattern is reproduced above.

### 11. Shared sleep can be loved, used temporarily, ineffective, painful or unwanted even when the baby sleeps well.

**Current findings:** §§5.5, 6.3 and 9.5. **Relationship:** Confirms and extends.

**Current conclusion:** “Some value the closeness, some want to keep it while changing feeds or naps, some cannot sleep that way, and some reject both cosleeping and crying methods.”

**What should change or stay:** Keep this as the organizing conclusion, then distinguish the desired combination: independent daytime naps while retaining shared nights; an initial crib stretch plus later shared sleep; temporary sharing during sickness or travel; keeping feeding while moving to the crib; or ending shared sleep despite twelve uninterrupted hours for the baby. Preserve contentment and closeness as outcomes in their own right. One parent calls cosleeping a tool rather than a rule; several explicitly say it does not solve waking, and others say it does. Do not turn these into stable buyer camps or assume a good child night means the adult wants the arrangement.

### 12. Night weaning, ending comfort feeding and ending breastfeeding are different decisions, constrained by growth, pain and upcoming events.

**Current findings:** §§3, 6.1–6.2 and 9.5. **Relationship:** Extends.

**Current conclusion:** “Restored questions ask which association to change first”

**What should change or stay:** Make the order and retained goals explicit. Parents want fewer comfort feeds while keeping bedsharing or daytime breastfeeding; some do not want full night weaning. Growth history and reported low intake make hunger uncertain even after independent settling; a parent whose child fell from above the ninetieth to the fifteenth percentile is reluctant to remove feeds. Parents discuss watered formula, delaying feeds and replacing bottles, with their reported advice and reasoning intact. A dream feed may merely move the next waking. Dental advice, illness, teething and a new sibling set other deadlines; none of this establishes a universal feed-removal rule.

### 13. Feeding mechanics, refusal and nursing behavior make removing an association more complicated than changing the final bedtime step.

**Current findings:** §§3, 4.5 and 6.1–6.2. **Relationship:** Extends.

**Current conclusion:** “the research conclusion is that health, feeding and access shape the sleep question.”

**What should change or stay:** Keep feeding technique and tolerability visible: painful pinching, latching and unlatching, refusing bottles, cows-milk transitions, daytime intake, and a baby whose weight and feeds do not fit a generic night schedule. Include a parent who delays milk containing pain medication until after crying so the child will not associate crying with parental submission. That account is a reported choice about method and care, rather than evidence that the rule works.

### 14. Upright holding, feeding difficulty and reflux workups can create the sleep association parents are later told to remove.

**Current findings:** §§3, 4.5, 9.1 and 17.2. **Relationship:** Extends.

**Current conclusion:** “whether a medical need is being mistaken for a habit.”

**What should change or stay:** Carry the feeding sequence, not just the word reflux. One mother reports two choking hospitalizations, a refused referral, bottle/nipple changes and then a week of improved feeding and longer sleep. Others report medication improving visible reflux while upright sleeping persists, no benefit from medication, gas after a trial, or symptoms appearing after an earlier schedule-success story. The upright interval itself can consume thirty-five to forty minutes after a feed. Preserve late replies that add medical information; they qualify attribution to a clock change. Bottle switches and multiple simultaneous changes are reported sequences, not proven mechanisms.

### 15. Allergies and itching can prevent independent sleep and change what parents consider an acceptable crying method.

**Current findings:** §§3, 4.5, 6.2 and 17.9. **Relationship:** Extends.

**Current conclusion:** “check the non-clock causes first”

**What should change or stay:** Add the concrete care task: a parent holds the baby’s arms while chest sleeping because of eczema; another asks whether CIO is acceptable while the eyes still itch. Other excerpts describe elimination diets, uncertain food triggers, specialist/insurance delays, persistent itching despite improved-looking skin, and allergic reactions limiting bottle alternatives. Keep the parent’s causal beliefs distinct from observed symptoms. The question is not solely how firmly to remove a prop.

### 16. Pain relief, sleep remedies and teething explanations produce mixed observations rather than one reliable explanation of waking.

**Current findings:** §§3, 4.6 and 17.2–17.9. **Relationship:** Confirms and extends.

**Current conclusion:** “medication and supplements that helped, failed or stopped helping”

**What should change or stay:** Keep both directions: Tylenol/Nurofen/Motrin sometimes precedes more sleep, sometimes leaves characteristic waking or crying unchanged, and sometimes is hard to administer. Parents question repeated dosing for an unconfirmed cause or ask for something to keep a toddler asleep; melatonin, magnesium and sleep drops also appear. One account reports better sleep during illness and worse after recovery elsewhere in this digest. These observations do not diagnose teething or establish that medication caused an outcome. Preserve every preparation, timing concern and uncertainty as the parent’s words.

### 17. Normal results, low-range iron, referral decisions and new symptoms leave the medical explanation unresolved in different ways.

**Current findings:** §§3, 6.2, 8 and 17.2. **Relationship:** Extends with contrary evidence.

**Current conclusion:** “a referral refused, tests, medication and supplements that helped, failed or stopped helping”

**What should change or stay:** Put the iron counterexamples beside the low-ferritin account already in findings: iron prescribed for nearly low but in-range ferritin did not help; another child has ferritin 24 and reportedly sleeps fine. Other tests are normal despite persistent waking. Respiratory signs, large tonsils, mouth breathing and family apnea history prompt workup and limit waiting through cries; an apparent daytime well child can still receive a report of inflamed ears and tonsils. Parents ask what list or referral route to take to a doctor. A result or a proposed diagnosis is not a confirmed cause of the sleep pattern.

### 18. Illness changes the night’s care workload, but recovery and sleep do not reliably move together.

**Current findings:** §§2, 3, 5.4 and 17.2. **Relationship:** Confirms and extends.

**Current conclusion:** “Illness, feeding, travel, a bed move and treatment changes accompany the return of waking.”

**What should change or stay:** Add the work inside the label: clearing congestion despite resistance, upright holding, ear/respiratory illness, fever, parents being ill at the same time, and losing reliable help because of infection risk. Preserve the outlier whose baby sleeps better while sick and worse when recovered. These passages qualify a simple respond-then-return sequence: the workload or uncertainty may persist, and parents ask for solidarity as well as an intervention.

### 19. Seeking appropriate help can fail at referral, routing, timing or trust before any sleep plan is tried.

**Current findings:** §§3, 8, 9.6 and 10. **Relationship:** Extends.

**Current conclusion:** “the cost of professional assessment”

**What should change or stay:** Broaden access beyond money. A primary-care appointment is unavailable until September after an OBGYN dismisses symptoms; perinatal services redirect someone because symptoms began later; developmental centers require a pediatrician’s referral despite PPO insurance; a family doctor will not refer for persistent sleep concerns. A changed insurance plan also ends a valued therapy relationship elsewhere in the digest. The demand is sometimes for usable care access or a script for being heard, rather than another schedule.

<!-- voice:sbu16-0045 -->

<!-- /voice -->

<!-- voice:sbu16-0204 -->

<!-- /voice -->

If he gets out in the crib, he’ll jump on the mattress over 200 times instead of lying down.  Crawl all over the couch at risk for flipping off, tackle the couch, throw stuff.  Cant sit still for a second when I try to read to him.  Then by 4-5pm, he starts to get really sleepy and can hardly stay awake.  But I can’t let him sleep at 5pm because then he’d wake up at 4am.  So we push his bedtime to 7-8pm.  And because he’s overtired, he’ll wake up screaming at 2am, then leak out of his diaper at 4am, which requires changing his diaper, PJs, sleep sac.

### 20. Paying for sleep help can mean buying an awake caregiver, whose failure can destroy trust despite a partial refund.

**Current findings:** §§4.1, 8, 10 and 17.3; add a night-care service subsection. **Relationship:** Extends.

**Current conclusion:** “But what parents say they paid for is a person, not information:”

**What should change or stay:** Do not limit the person to advice, reassurance or accountability. The night nanny was hired, with a referral and checks, to stay awake holding a sick baby upright and provide frequent feeds. The parent reports the nanny fell asleep, defended herself with maternal experience and refunded only the hour she probably slept. Keep the stated care requirement, the defense, refund and subsequent refusal to trust a stranger together. The fee is not given. A document cannot substitute for the purchased awake hands in this account.

<!-- voice:sbu17-0038 -->

### 21. Independent bedtime, independent naps, night resettling, feeding and adult recovery are separate outcomes.

**Current findings:** §§2, 4.4–4.5, 6.1 and 17.2. **Relationship:** Extends.

**Current conclusion:** “a result that does not survive the first setback”

**What should change or stay:** Not every incomplete result is a later relapse: for some, bedtime succeeds while night wakings never improve. A parent asks for training specific to a child who already self-settles but fails in one night window; another questions the feed gate once bedtime is solved. A successful first night or night four can coexist with poor naps, and responsive no-cry settling also succeeds in a reported case. Count changes in each outcome separately and preserve the parent’s definition of success. A pleasant bedtime is not evidence of consolidated nights.

Night wakes always remained a nightmare as we really struggled with him crying for extended periods of time - he would also SCREAM not just fuss. So I’m aware that we were pretty inconsistent in our approach with intermittent teething and illness making things worse in that respect. Between 6 and 10 months we went through periods of rocking him to sleep, then doing sleep training and reinforcing independent settling at bedtime (which normally was quick and effective). But night wakes never really improved. We have seen him self settling in the night, but once he’s properly up he really struggles going back to sleep using any training methods. He’s exclusively breastfed and I would normally revert to feeding him for our own sanity.

### 22. Parents stop or modify attempts because of the child’s physical distress, injury risk, their own limit or a rule they cannot apply.

**Current findings:** §§4.5, 6.1 and 17.6–17.9. **Relationship:** Confirms and extends.

**Current conclusion:** “These are concrete accounts of why a method ended or was ruled out, rather than evidence that persistence alone resolves it.”

**What should change or stay:** Add the exact limits: breath holding and turning purple, respiratory symptoms with a nebulizer, vomiting and gagging, lost voice, sweating through sheets, head banging, climbing, and check-ins or repeated pick-up/put-down that escalate distress. One parent says they cannot hear more crying at every nap while nights are being trained. Parents explicitly ask for intervals, crying caps, what to do about rolling/standing and whether they should have kept going. A stop rule is not just a longer timer; it must distinguish the reported care need and caregiver capacity. Keep actual short successes and mixed choices alongside failures.

### 23. Schedules and methods can stop fitting after illness, travel, development or a nap change, and the old method may no longer work.

**Current findings:** §§2, 4.4, 6.1 and 17.2. **Relationship:** Confirms and extends.

**Current conclusion:** “these are observed sequences rather than proven causes or a universal restart protocol.”

**What should change or stay:** Preserve both brief apparent wins and longer histories. Compare one good schedule day with a disastrous second day, a month of consolidated nights followed by a two-nap transition, travel requiring two weeks to regain independent settling, and an old training method that now escalates crying. The early-waking parent reports that moving to one nap made wakes even earlier; another is pushing noon despite exhausted cues because of what is supposed to happen. A later medical discovery can change the explanation of an earlier free fix. Do not infer the cause or prescribe one restart interval from these sequences.

### 24. Parents want workable next steps while uncertain whether the issue is timing, separation, regulation or the method itself.

**Current findings:** §§4.4, 6.1–6.2 and 17.6. **Relationship:** Confirms and extends.

**Current conclusion:** “They ask for a plan for this child, written as steps, with a rule for when things go wrong.”

**What should change or stay:** Retain straightforward schedule and method requests alongside crisis accounts. Requests include which nap to cap, how to handle an accidental car nap, when to try the crib, whether to keep contact naps, what happens on a standing or rolling wake, and how to preserve an evening. The explicit poop-at-nap account does not change when the clock moves across a two-hour window; after no poop the toddler sleeps three hours. That is a concrete counterexample to treating all nap refusal as timing. The giant soothing the baby is a perspective piece rather than an outcome trial and is labelled in the advice pattern.

### 25. Parents cannot apply age guidance without deciding how prematurity, this child’s cues and actual sleep change the comparison.

**Current findings:** §§2 and 6.2, with contrary evidence in §17.6. **Relationship:** Extends.

**Current conclusion:** “Age alone is not enough.”

**What should change or stay:** Name chronological versus adjusted age explicitly. A seventeen-week-old is twelve weeks adjusted; another nearly seven-month-old is treated as five months. Conversely, a consultant’s adjusted-age ninety-minute windows make a preterm child cry more. A ten-month-old sleeps twelve-plus uninterrupted hours after dropping to one nap and the parent questions shortening those nights to fit forum age guidance. Preserve that as an individual counterexample, not a replacement age table. Unusually long sleep and very young babies sleeping long stretches also prompt questions rather than universal goals.

### 26. Sleepwear, sleep spaces and monitors are repeated experiments whose fit, lifespan and usability affect value.

**Current findings:** §§4.6, 5.4 and 8. **Relationship:** Confirms and extends.

**Current conclusion:** “temporary wins, failures, temperature and daycare constraints, and repeated reluctance to buy another short-lived fix.”

**What should change or stay:** Add the concrete value question: whether rails, cribs or extendable beds last long enough to justify another purchase and fit the room. One swaddle transition takes about twelve days before later eleven-to-twelve-hour nights; another parent continues swaddling after rolling because every alternative has failed. Crib height, mattress thickness, rail height, moving feet, socks and TOG uncertainty generate ordinary shopping and setup requests. A monitoring feature is neither uniformly helpful nor uniformly frightening. Keep purchase reports separate from product possession, recommendations and discount arithmetic.

### 27. Daycare and relatives can constrain naps and care, provide relief or be difficult to trust.

**Current findings:** §§5.3–5.4, 6.2 and 9.6. **Relationship:** Confirms and extends.

**Current conclusion:** “another concrete limit on a family’s control of the plan.”

**What should change or stay:** Preserve the specific care disagreement, not only the nap schedule: a child is left face down on a cushioned mat; staff call no-nap crying something to break; relatives disregard requested windows or press their own sleep arrangements. Other relatives provide restorative coverage, while abuse history makes an offered grandmother shift hard to use. Daycare sleep can succeed despite home contact sleeping, and illness after entry can undo the plan. A replacement nanny or professional opinion has its own access and trust conditions elsewhere in this digest.

### 28. Space, ventilation, heat, siblings and travel determine which otherwise plausible sleep arrangements can be attempted.

**Current findings:** §§4.5, 8 and 9.6. **Relationship:** Confirms and extends.

**Current conclusion:** “standard advice, which assumes space, a partner or control of the schedule”

**What should change or stay:** Keep the physical constraints visible: a basement door cannot simply be closed because of ventilation; three people overheat in a double bed; twins share the bedroom and crawl back from a floor mattress; two extendable beds are expensive and large; a family already bought its bed and cannot immediately fund a floor bed. Travel raises extra-seat, car-seat, crib and mattress questions. A landlord telling a parent to ignore a neighbour is a counterexample to assuming every complaint blocks training. These are setup decisions as well as method decisions.

### 29. Sleep hardship changes plans for another child and the preparation parents seek, as well as whether an old method fits the next baby.

**Current findings:** §§9.1 and 9.3, with a preparation subsection in §6.1. **Relationship:** Extends.

**Current conclusion:** “They know the methods. What failed is the fit.”

**What should change or stay:** Do not leave experienced-parent needs at method transfer. A recovered parent credits Zoloft and time and asks how to protect the family in another postpartum period; others dread another colicky baby, feel relief at not restarting, or need a combined toddler/newborn plan while a partner works nights. Some want another pregnancy after bereavement; that distinct account is preserved in the loss pattern. Sibling noise, mismatched naps and impending birth create practical deadlines without proving that training is the answer.

**[sbu09-0212]**

<!-- voice:sbu09-0212 -->
> It got so bad at one point I had intrusive thoughts of jumping out a window or of harming my baby. I never ever \*desired\* to harm her, in fact my thoughts were mostly absolutely consumed with how to protect her - but I was so severely sleep deprived an mentally unwell that I think my subconscious knew I wasn’t fit to protect her, and it was almost like my brian was looking for a way to get out of the endless loop of delirium from severe sleep deprivation, the stress of a colicky newborn on top of that, and the extreme isolation and lack of support I felt. 
> 
> Important info: my husband, while not perfect, is truly wonderful and does / did everything he could to support me. He is not the problem, nor is a lack of social support. At the end of the day, it just is what it is when baby has severe reflux, screams 75% of the time she’s awake, and only sleeps in 30 min increments.

### 30. Parents question advice systems and harm claims, including chatbots, rather than simply needing more instructions.

**Current findings:** §§3, 4.2–4.3, 7 and 17.5–17.6. **Relationship:** Confirms and extends.

**Current conclusion:** “Where it appears it is usually blamed:”

**What should change or stay:** Add the parent who abandons conflicting ChatGPT advice, the overly supportive Gemini that helped a parent misunderstand Ferber, and the GPT wake-window advice that clashes with visibly exhausted cues. Preserve criticism of consultants as predatory/fake science as the parent’s belief. Family accusations of abuse, questions comparing illness crying with training crying, and the voiced giant-soothing story are perspectives rather than established attachment or clinical findings. A source flagged possible_promo stays attributed and is not promoted into independent corroboration.

**[sbu09-0271]**

<!-- voice:sbu09-0271 -->
> Please help me! Idk what else to do. ChatGPT gives conflicting info so I don’t even use it anymore and I think sleep consultants are predatory and fake science. (Sorry to any sleep consultants in here). Any tips or ways to help my child link her freaking sleep cycles and independently fall asleep?
<!-- /voice -->

**[sbu09-0196]**

> What are your kids doing? I’ve asked chat gpt and sick of its advice!!!
<!-- /voice -->

I started Ferber two weeks ago, but didn't realize I wasn't doing it right the first few nights (relied too much on feedback from an overly-supportive Gemini - lesson learned). An infant crying and parents not “fixing” the reason for crying. ***I*** understand they’re two vastly different things, as I have control over him crying alone in his crib versus having no control over his esophageal function, but an infant doesn’t know that. ## Outliers and counter-examples

These passages qualify the patterns above. Some are deliberately repeated in full so the contrary words remain visible beside the interpretation. None is a prevalence estimate.

### Counter-example 1

Training is reported to reduce dangerous accidental chair sleep and improve everyone’s rest; keep this alongside harm/regret accounts.

**[sbu09-0033]**

<!-- voice:sbu09-0033 -->
> That’s not necessarily true that no harm could come. I was up hourly with him overnights and I would often fall asleep with him in my arms in the rocking chair accidentally because I was so tired. That could easily become a dangerous situation as it was not safe cosleeping. With the training, he sleeps through the night and so do I. We are all much better with proper rest
<!-- /voice -->

### Counter-example 2

The newborn/parenthood experience is not uniformly miserable: one mother says she loves motherhood including the hard parts.

**[sbu09-0206]**

<!-- voice:sbu09-0206 -->
> my baby is the light of my life, i wake up so excited to see her little smile, i love playing with her, i love contact napping with her, i just love being a mom, even the hard parts.
<!-- /voice -->

### Counter-example 3

Better nights can accompany meaningful adult recovery, rather than only persistent symptoms.

**[sbu16-0142]**

<!-- voice:sbu16-0142 -->
> We're all sleeping through the night and she takes a regular one-hour nap a day. We're back to working out and making some of our meals. We even have energy to go out on date nights/see friends.
<!-- /voice -->

**[sbu16-0357]**

<!-- voice:sbu16-0357 -->
> My son just started sleeping through the night, and is in his own room now, so life is finally starting to feel a little more manageable. At the same time, staying home with a toddler all day is also a lot, and I think that is something only other parents really understand.
<!-- /voice -->

**[sbu17-0283]**

<!-- voice:sbu17-0283 -->
> My mental health has been very, very bad and has only recently gotten better now that I’m sleeping through the night again since baby is only waking up once and
<!-- /voice -->

### Counter-example 4

Supportive partners exist, and substantial cooperation still does not guarantee the parent copes well.

**[sbu17-0185]**

<!-- voice:sbu17-0185 -->
> He lets me sleep as much as I need while he takes her for morning walks, feeds her, changes her diapers, helps with bath time, and takes her off my hands whenever he can so I can get a break.
<!-- /voice -->

**[sbu16-0118]**

<!-- voice:sbu16-0118 -->
> Bear in mind my husband is super hands on. It’s a true 50/50 split of responsibilities and mental load but were both just feel like we’re going through a washing machine on the daily and there’s no relief. Does it get better at a certain age???
<!-- /voice -->

**[sbu09-0212]**

<!-- voice:sbu09-0212 -->
> It got so bad at one point I had intrusive thoughts of jumping out a window or of harming my baby. I never ever \*desired\* to harm her, in fact my thoughts were mostly absolutely consumed with how to protect her - but I was so severely sleep deprived an mentally unwell that I think my subconscious knew I wasn’t fit to protect her, and it was almost like my brian was looking for a way to get out of the endless loop of delirium from severe sleep deprivation, the stress of a colicky newborn on top of that, and the extreme isolation and lack of support I felt. 
> 
> Important info: my husband, while not perfect, is truly wonderful and does / did everything he could to support me. He is not the problem, nor is a lack of social support. At the end of the day, it just is what it is when baby has severe reflux, screams 75% of the time she’s awake, and only sleeps in 30 min increments.
<!-- /voice -->

### Counter-example 5

Some severe distress coexists with relatively good infant sleep or is explicitly said to be unrelated to the baby.

**[sbu16-0187]**

<!-- voice:sbu16-0187 -->
> Our baby is honestly….so easy compared to all the stories we heard about how it might be. We get good sleep. She naps throughout the day so I have time to do things. She’s great in the car and on walks. It really isn’t related to anything with the baby.
<!-- /voice -->

**[sbu16-0200]**

<!-- voice:sbu16-0200 -->
> In terms of sleep, 1-2 night feeds still.
> 
> I cry every day, I go to bed hoping I don’t wake up. Every evening I put baby in a safe place and have to walk away for 5 minutes as it’s unbearable.
<!-- /voice -->

**[sbu17-0158]**

<!-- voice:sbu17-0158 -->
> My son just turned 10 months old and has started napping appropriately and sleeping through the night 99% of the time. I thought my mental health would improve but the opposite is happening.
<!-- /voice -->

### Counter-example 6

A parent explicitly rejects the offered anxiety framing and says the problem is lack of sleep.

**[sbu09-0111]**

<!-- voice:sbu09-0111 -->
> I’ve talked to my doctor & all he can reccomend is anxiety medicine but I genuinely don’t feel like that’s the issue. I don’t have anxiety i just am not sleeping. how do I help the exhaustion??
<!-- /voice -->

### Counter-example 7

Iron observations cut in different directions; do not turn low-range ferritin into a single explanation.

**[sbu09-0261]**

<!-- voice:sbu09-0261 -->
> Glad yall found a solution that worked. Unfortunately, we tried everything. Lots of natural light, lots of activity both mentally and physically demanding, longer naps, shorter naps, earlier naps, later naps. We went to a pediatrician a few times about the sleep and they agreed it was abnormal. Kiddo's ferritin was in range but was nearly low so they gave her iron drops which didn't help.
<!-- /voice -->

**[sbu09-0441]**

<!-- voice:sbu09-0441 -->
> My child just got their 24 month bloodwork done and ferritin level was 24. The range says 20-70. It’s on the lower end, her CBC was totally normal and in range. I saw some posts saying it’s ideal to have a level above 35? She sleeps totally fine and eats well.
<!-- /voice -->

### Counter-example 8

The calm-newborn/autism account explicitly rejects a universal inference.

**[sbu16-0352]**

<!-- voice:sbu16-0352 -->
> Our son was a dream newborn. Slept great, never cried (hint hint), ate well, all around unicorn. I've been able to get a full nights sleep since he was \~10 weeks old. Now, I look back and while certainly grateful, he was probably a bit too calm. It was almost as if he didn't realize he could show that he was upset about something, he lacked that very early communication skill. **Here is where I point out that I don't think all calm babies are autistic and many parents of autistic children report that theirs's were especially difficult babies. Autism being a spectrum doesn't even begin to explain how varied the disorder is.**
<!-- /voice -->

### Counter-example 9

Falling asleep beside a parent was long compatible with good sleep; a later issue does not prove that onset arrangement was always the cause.

**[sbu09-0437]**

<!-- voice:sbu09-0437 -->
> She sleeps on a double floor bed in her own room and has done happily since around 16m. Didn’t change anything about her sleep when we switched from a cot and has been great. She falls asleep with one of us laying next to her, usually only takes around 10-15 mins. We’re happy with that situation and it’s worked for as long as we can remember now and never contributed to disrupted sleep from falling asleep that way.
<!-- /voice -->

**[sbu09-0438]**

<!-- voice:sbu09-0438 -->
> Over the past 2 weeks, our toddler has figured out how to open the door to her bedroom. We discovered this when we were woken up by it in the night, and she appeared in our room and climbed into our bed (she’s never coslept!). 
> 
> We’ve tried to be consistent and put her back in her own room, but she either will not settle, or needs one of us to stay with her or she’ll wake again. This will be anywhere between 11pm and 4:30am. Sometimes we can sneak back out and she’ll sleep for another few hours before coming back to our room again.
<!-- /voice -->

### Counter-example 10

Shared sleep can yield twelve uninterrupted hours yet remain unwanted; home shared sleep can coexist with independent daycare sleep.

**[sbu17-0263]**

<!-- voice:sbu17-0263 -->
> He sleeps AMAZING in bed with us. Will sleep a full 12 hours with no wakings. I don’t want to co-sleep though.
<!-- /voice -->

**[sbu17-0016]**

<!-- voice:sbu17-0016 -->
> I breastfed past 1 year, we still cosleep and we love it (and amazingly enough, he sleeps like a dream in creche!), I respond to his needs immediately. All the other parents I spoke have children who took the bottle from an extremely young age if not from birth, were sleep trained and sleep alone in their room already, falling asleep by themselves.
<!-- /voice -->

### Counter-example 11

Responsive settling without a crying method is reported to work, and the parent values even a short nap.

**[sbu17-0012]**

<!-- voice:sbu17-0012 -->
> I am sitting in front of the crib and LO has napped “awake but sleepy” without me for the first time ever.
> 
> After 3 months of exclusively contact napping. No tears, no crying. Bub was laughing and babbling until they were tired enough and fell asleep with my hand on their chest and caressing their cheek (30m past their wake window).
<!-- /voice -->

**[sbu17-0013]**

<!-- voice:sbu17-0013 -->
> I want to jump for joy. I feel so connect to Bub that we achieved this together. Not from crying and being left alone. But with Mum and trusting mum is always here. I will take 10m nap or a 2hr nap. It doesn’t matter. I will contact nap whenever required.
<!-- /voice -->

### Counter-example 12

Age guidance and adjusted-age guidance do not fit every observed child.

**[sbu09-0396]**

<!-- voice:sbu09-0396 -->
> The first day she refused her 2nd nap, I PANICKED. Thinking she would be ridiculously overtired by bedtime, multiple night wakes. We followed her cues that night and ended up putting her in the crib at 7:25pm, she fell asleep at 7:30pm. We had to wake her at 8am. She slept 12.5 hours straight! The next day, she was slightly sleepier that night and ended up asleep by 7:15pm. She woke up at 7:55am. 12.75 hours of straight sleep at night with zero wakes.
<!-- /voice -->

**[sbu09-0397]**

<!-- voice:sbu09-0397 -->
> Everyone on Reddit recommends to max out a 2 nap schedule before dropping to 1 nap, and cutting nights down to 10.5 hours to fit in both naps, but why would I cut out sleep from my baby’s wonderful nights? Is there truly a reason I need to do this or dropping to 1 nap at this age will screw her up?
<!-- /voice -->

**[sbu16-0243]**

<!-- voice:sbu16-0243 -->
> People often use adjusted rather than actual age for preemie sleep, but that doesn’t seem to work for him. We hired the consultant to help us sleep train in mid March (6 actual, 4.5 adjusted), and she wanted us to use 90min wake windows. He screamed & cried so much more before naps.
<!-- /voice -->

### Counter-example 13

A difficult swaddle transition resolves after about twelve days without another transitional product; the later follow-up reports eleven-to-twelve-hour nights.

**[sbu09-0076]**

<!-- voice:sbu09-0076 -->
> Hi! It lasted about 12 or so days. It was horrible, but it passed! I just stuck to it. No transitional swaddles or anything else.  My now 5.5 mo old baby sleeps 11-12 hours through the night with 0 wakes! He learned to suck his thumb in the transition and that’s how he soothes himself and puts him to sleep independently! So it ended up being for the best! You’ll get through it!
<!-- /voice -->

### Counter-example 14

Illness can accompany better sleep and recovery worse sleep, cutting against a simple directional illness narrative.

**[sbu17-0224]**

<!-- voice:sbu17-0224 -->
> Then she got sick and started sleeping GREAT! she cried a little before bed, then only woke up once to eat. But now she is better again and back to crap sleep.
<!-- /voice -->

### Counter-example 15

The monitor microphone is a striking success in one account and frightens a child in another.

**[sbu16-0034]**

<!-- voice:sbu16-0034 -->
> My son(17m) has been waking up between 4-5 AM standing and screaming… This morning I used our monitors microphone function for the first time ever and just said “I love you lay back down”…. AND HE DID?? it worked TWICE??? It’s currently 7:02 and I’m still laying in bed. It feels like a miracle 😭 why did I not try this before?
<!-- /voice -->

**[sbu17-0305]**

<!-- voice:sbu17-0305 -->
> Talked through the camera 1mo ago and freaked him out, causing him to feel scared and anxious.  Dropped the nap.  Not afraid of the camera anymore.
<!-- /voice -->

### Counter-example 16

A landlord supports ignoring a neighbour complaint rather than ending the attempt.

**[sbu17-0304]**

<!-- voice:sbu17-0304 -->
> Yea I actually already talked to our landlord and she told me to ignore her if she continues to complain
<!-- /voice -->

### Counter-example 17

An illness-related need for rocking can end with return to independent settling; not every illness means training from scratch.

**[sbu09-0355]**

<!-- voice:sbu09-0355 -->
> He got sick a bunch and has been teething endlessly (8 teeth at 7 months) and when he's not well he will not fall asleep on his own. He will work himself up til he's so upset that he's gasping and screaming. so eventually I started rocking him to sleep after 10 minutes or less of upset when he's unwell.
> 
> He will still return to putting himself to sleep once he feels better.
<!-- /voice -->

### Counter-example 18

Regret and perceived benefit from the same training attempt coexist in one parent’s assessment.

**[sbu09-0342]**

<!-- voice:sbu09-0342 -->
> part of me wishes I never did it, because I later learned that my baby was actually probably in a lot of pain and terror in those moments, and IMO you wouldn’t ignore anyone screaming in pain. But part of me is glad I did do it- because I would’ve always been wondering and hemming and hawwing over whether or not to. And it showed me I could do hard things, and I could do it again if I needed to. Do it, or don’t. There’s not a right or wrong answer here.
<!-- /voice -->

### Counter-example 19

A new toddler bed is liked and sleep improves in one account; other bed moves leave sleep unresolved.

**[sbu16-0345]**

<!-- voice:sbu16-0345 -->
> Here's the thing, we spent the past week at my in-laws and they got him a little toddler bed for their place and he LOVED it. Asked for naps all day because he wanted to use it haha! He slept until 6 every day.
<!-- /voice -->

**[sbu09-0421]**

<!-- voice:sbu09-0421 -->
> Most recently we moved him to a toddler bed (with high railings), to see if it would help and also so that we could just get in bed and sleep with him when he wakes. This has been an improvement over holding him for over an hour in the gilder, but he STILL will be awake for like 1.5 hours!! Rolling around, talking, then sucking his thumb and trying to go to sleep. It’s like he just can’t!!
<!-- /voice -->

### Counter-example 20

Family care can provide a real six-hour rest even while the mother feels guilty about using it.

**[sbu09-0174]**

<!-- voice:sbu09-0174 -->
> My boyfriends mom moved in a few months ago to help. She heard me crying and my baby crying and scooped him out of bed and told me to sleep. I felt so bad, I cried to her. She told me not to worry and just get some sleep. 
> 
> I slept for about 6 hours. I woke up to my boyfriend and his mom talking while they were changing my baby's diaper. It honestly felt really good to sleep without worrying about my baby, but I also feel so bad for having someone watch him while I slept instead of get things done like I normally do
<!-- /voice -->

## What fits nowhere

These are on-niche passages. The current contents have broad headings that can hold them, but no dedicated subsection for the mechanism or event. The suggested placements are new subdivisions, not reasons to omit the evidence.

### Infant bereavement and future pregnancy

There is no bereavement section. §6.4 currently organizes death mainly as fear; add reported loss and connect it to future-family preparation without treating the event as merely a fear quote.

**[sbu16-0359]**

<!-- voice:sbu16-0359 -->
> i lost my son 16 days ago, he was only 14 days old, (due to co sleeping accident i talk about it in another post)
<!-- /voice -->

**[sbu16-0360]**

<!-- voice:sbu16-0360 -->
> my brain feels so guilty for even thinking when can i try again, and then i say how could i EVER have another baby when i want him. then i say why the fuck am i even deserving of that!!!! but i know i crave being a mom more than anything... that's what i was supposed to be doing right now is loving my beautiful son. the question is when's too early? i feel so fucking guilty for having these thoughts, i don't want to betray him, bc NOTHING can ever replace him, every second my feelings are something different. i can't believe this our reality, i can't believe my sweet son doesn't get to be enduring the love my partner and i had for our little family, i feel so sick that he can't experience life again, he should be here...  
> then i think whenever of the fear i'm going to feel the rest of my life when i have other babies and how ill always worry something will happen no matter what i do... idk if any of this makes sense. it's been a hard day. he would of been one month old today.... it will never feel real.
<!-- /voice -->

### Two adult/infant clocks that do not line up

Pumping/oversupply and drying supply are not given their own mechanism in the current sleep-stage or parental-sleep sections. Add a lactation-clock subsection under §§3/9.1.

**[sbu09-0095]**

<!-- voice:sbu09-0095 -->
> It’s not even just that my baby won’t let me put her down. She also wants fed about every 2-2.5 hours, so even though she’s exceeded her birth weight and we technically can let her sleep longer, she won’t, not that it’s even relevant since that sleep is in my arms. I also have to pump my massive oversupply of breast milk at minimum every 4 hours, which I can’t even feed my baby because she doesn’t tolerate it, so it’s just another 24 hour demanding schedule that I have to follow just to fill up my deep freezer and maybe donate later or something. 
> 
> I’m just so frustrated. Between the feeding schedule and pumping schedule that both have to be set very rigidity, and baby being so difficult to set down, I just feel like sleep is impossible. On the off chance that I do get her to sleep independently, that window will not be longer than an hour or so and if it doesn’t fall between pumping then it’s useless to try and sleep.
<!-- /voice -->

**[sbu09-0096]**

<!-- voice:sbu09-0096 -->
> I was pumping to bottle feed breast milk before we had to switch to formula and now I’m slowly backing off the pumping to dry my supply. Right now I produce 10 oz in a 15 minute pump so I can’t stop cold turkey without risking infection =\\ but I’m definitely trying to get off the pump. I hate it
<!-- /voice -->

**[sbu16-0216]**

<!-- voice:sbu16-0216 -->
> how did you manage with engorged breasts? were you ever worried about mastitis?
<!-- /voice -->

### Medication-start coordination rather than an ordinary sleep transition

The current health headings do not provide a place for a planned short treatment course that changes safe caregiving capacity, shifts and milk logistics at once. Add a coordination subsection under §10’s fit limits.

**[sbu17-0100]**

<!-- voice:sbu17-0100 -->
> Since about three weeks postpartum, I’ve had to chest sleep with my newborn. I follow the safe sleep seven rules. I never thought I’d bed share/cosleep like this, but here we are. The first few weeks of endless failed bassinet transfers and pure exhaustion got us here. But now I’m about to begin a medication, zurzuvae, which will likely come with significant fatigue and heavy, heavy sleep as a side effect, which obviously makes it incompatible with chest sleeping.
<!-- /voice -->

**[sbu17-0101]**

<!-- voice:sbu17-0101 -->
> I was thinking about taking the med at like 5-6pm, conking out for three hours at a time, and just having my husband wake me every three hours to pump (or nurse directly if I’m coordinated and not too out of it to hold baby. I will be on a firm floor mattress, btw). I have a milk stash if my husband needs to feed the babe during the time I’m out sleeping. After a full six hours of sleep, I’d then go ahead and let my husband sleep, and I’d then stay up with baby. Although, that 12am-6am shift is when I’m most scared of falling asleep unsafely with our babe. My husband is already having to be back at work full time. Luckily, he does work remotely at home.
<!-- /voice -->

**[sbu17-0102]**

<!-- voice:sbu17-0102 -->

> 
> Chestsleeping, we’re usually getting 2.5-3.5 **hour** stretches of continuous sleep, and that’s really been the only thing that’s helped me survive the past several weeks.
<!-- /voice -->

### Buying awake hands and the terms of failed overnight care

The consultant/course/accountability structure does not adequately describe a nanny hired to remain awake. Add a separate overnight-care service category in §§4/8.

There is no subsection for the repeated poop-after-putdown pattern. Keep it as a specific nap-counterexample under §4.4 rather than another unsupported timing diagnosis.

### Preparing for a feared repeat of severe postpartum illness

§9.3 centers on failed transfer of an old sleep method. The request here is how to protect a new baby, toddler and parents before another pregnancy/postpartum period; add that distinct preparation request while retaining §9.1’s existing family-planning evidence.

>

## Money

The three quote records with currency amounts are the discount breakdown [sbu16-0132], nursery crib/mattress spending and an additional-spend ceiling [sbu16-0147], and the mattress budget [sbu17-0011]. Their complete words are reproduced above. The discount recommendation is not proof of a purchase. The night-nanny refund gives an hour, not a fee or refund amount. These records do not establish willingness to pay for Mamivio’s proposed document or price.

The first index records explicit money, transactions, returns, budgets, affordability and economic consequences. The second preserves relevant unpriced acquisitions, product trials and service use so that owning/trying a product, consulting someone or hoping to obtain something is not silently recast as a paid purchase. Dimensions, sleep durations and travel times are not prices. Every indexed record is reproduced in full in the patterns.

The Money index preserves the pressure to avoid another short-lived purchase, failed utilization of already bought equipment, insurance barriers and a service refund. It does not turn every remedy mention or professional visit into spending evidence.

## Requests

Every indexed passage is reproduced completely in the patterns above. The main table records direct product/setup, plan, instruction, practical-care and service/support asks; it also retains explicit diagnostic and perspective questions when that is the help sought. Asking for a remedy or a plan is not willingness to buy one. The separate context table preserves goals, proposed plans, service-access attempts and contemplated requests that must not be counted as outright product demand. No request for a paid Mamivio document is demonstrated here.

### Direct asks and practical questions

## Verification and coverage

The quote files were read in complete, consecutive record ranges: UR09 1–85, 86–170, 171–255, 256–340 and 341–431; UR16 1–100, 101–200, 201–300 and 301–349; UR17 1–86, 87–172, 173–258 and 259–344. No file or tail was sampled or skipped. Each ledger’s What these posts add section was read.

Programmatic checks loaded the three JSONL files into an ID-to-record map, independently parsed all 965 voice quotation blocks from this written digest, removed only the added Markdown blockquote prefix, and compared the recovered strings with the complete quote fields using exact equality. Result: 965 quotation occurrences, 896 distinct quote IDs, zero mismatches. No spelling, punctuation, case, Unicode or whitespace normalization was used in comparison. Repeated occurrences are not additional quote records.

A separate scan of every voice-style ID anywhere in this file found exactly the 1,124 IDs in this share: zero unknown IDs and zero unmentioned IDs. The 228 further supporting IDs are distinct, valid and disjoint from the 896 reproduced IDs. All Money and Requests index entries have complete quotations in the patterns. All 30 quoted current-conclusion excerpts were also checked literally against synthesis/findings.md. These checks validate transcription and share membership, not the speakers’ medical or causal claims.

Only synthesis/unfilter-digest-02.md was written. No network or git was used.

Coverage: quotes in the share: **1,124**; quotes reproduced verbatim: **896 distinct IDs** (**965 occurrences**); further IDs listed: **228 distinct IDs**; IDs not mentioned at all: **0**.
